The daily field guide for digital media

AI Media Scout

2026-11-13 · All platforms · Open-source software

An expanded open-ended search across creative AI, with full guides completed from earlier screened entries and additional eligible discoveries retained in the searchable pending-profile appendix. This is documentation/source review; no discovered application was installed or run. First profiles do not mean a launch today. Quality labels reflect evidence, not popularity. Incomplete evidence remains Quality unverified; current prototype or unfinished-state evidence is required for Experimental. Software, model weights, assets, hosted services and required proprietary hosts are distinguished. All platforms are researched; each guide reports the supported route rather than inferring universal compatibility. Verified collection: 887/887 repository queries attempted; 887 successful, 0 partial, 0 failed and 85 bounded/truncated. All 25 model tasks were attempted; 0 failed. These are bounded discovery attempts, not an exhaustive census or quality approval. Empty model-source responses: text2text-generation. An empty response does not establish that these creative workflows have no tools. Separate from successful search queries, 45 watchlist fetches and 3 enrichment attempts failed; the detailed warnings remain in the report. A 404/409 is an availability gap, not proof of abandonment or license revocation. Collection was resumed after an execution interruption before an observation was saved. Proven retained query/model responses and hash-matched README captures were reused with their capture times; the one missing repository query was attempted normally. Remaining repository, release and commit checks resumed live. No sealed edition or email was rebuilt or resent.

Search and review counts

102 creative fields; 887/887 repository queries attempted; 25/25 model-task queries attempted. 56545 distinct source candidates and 1075 model leads. 41 detailed profiles and 159 additional screened discoveries. Source gaps: 0 failed repository queries, 0 partial repository queries, 85 bounded repository queries, 0 failed model-task queries and 0 web ecosystem gaps. Raw search candidates include duplicates of known tools, excluded projects and projects awaiting review; they are not verified recommendations.

Profiles are based on current primary documentation. Creative quality and performance claims are attributed to the developers unless hands-on testing is explicitly recorded. Unknown hardware requirements stay unknown. Code, model weights, hosted services, and required proprietary hosts can have different terms.

Platforms
Documented Linux NVIDIA, native Windows 10/11 x64, Apple Silicon MPS and a specific Linux Strix Halo ROCm setup. The AMD recipe is not support for every AMD GPU; CUDA training/evaluation scripts are not generally ported to Mac.

Documentation review only; AI Media Scout did not install or run this software. VRM export currently accepts VRM 0.x rather than VRM 1.0. A plain GLB does not carry standardized spring physics. Card geometry and a successful export do not establish strand-level realism or production-ready simulation.

How it uses AI

A separate ComfyUI integration runs HairDeck’s image-conditioned diffusion pipeline and exposes hair-card generation, color, spring-bone chains, GLB and VRM export as graph nodes. [1] [3] [4]

Introduction

A separate ComfyUI integration runs HairDeck’s image-conditioned diffusion pipeline and exposes hair-card generation, color, spring-bone chains, GLB and VRM export as graph nodes. [1] [3] [4]

What it is good for

Generate editable stylized hair ribbons from a permitted character image and inspect static or rigged exports in an existing ComfyUI workflow. [1] [3] [4]

Demo & examples

The author supplies an example graph, node descriptions and GPU measurements. The implementation calls HairDeck inference and exports in nodes.py; Scout inspected the source rather than running or visually grading an output. [1] [3] [4]

Install

Place the official repository in ComfyUI/custom_nodes, install requirements with that ComfyUI’s own Python, then restart. Accept the separate SAM 3/DINOv3 gated terms before model access. Rigged GLB/VRM additionally need a matching bpy package or Blender executable; the documented Python 3.13 example uses bpy 5.2.3. [1] [3] [4]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.

python -m pip install -r ComfyUI/custom_nodes/ComfyUI-HairDeck/requirements.txt

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3] [4]

  1. Load the supplied example graph, supply a permitted portrait and use the model loader’s documented offload setting.
  2. Generate one variation and inspect its mask, card colors, geometry and spring chains before saving.
  3. Choose static GLB, rigged GLB or VRM. For your own VRM avatar use the separate avatar-loading node and inspect its head/hair mesh requirements; configure spring physics in the destination engine.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3] [4]

Hardware
The documented default is offload enabled. Model components total about 7.7 GB before Blender/environment/media; this is not total disk space. The author reports 6.7 GB peak VRAM in one offload test and about 10 GB without offload; neither is a universal minimum. System RAM is unspecified.
Software
An existing compatible ComfyUI/Python/PyTorch CUDA environment, git and the listed requirements. Matching bpy or an external Blender is required for rigged/VRM exports. Required model downloads and Blender are separate from the node package.
Platforms
The integration explicitly requires an NVIDIA GPU. Its developer test used an RTX 3080 Laptop 16 GB, Python 3.13, PyTorch 2.10 and CUDA 13. Standalone HairDeck’s MPS/ROCm support does not establish this plugin’s Mac/AMD compatibility.

How it uses AI

A model-assisted script-to-illustration workflow obtains static images and neural narration, then computes line tracing, region coloring and FFmpeg composition into a hand-drawing-style video. [1] [3]

Introduction

A model-assisted script-to-illustration workflow obtains static images and neural narration, then computes line tracing, region coloring and FFmpeg composition into a hand-drawing-style video. [1] [3]

What it is good for

Make an editable illustrated explainer with staged narration, per-scene images, draw-on timing and separately inspected captions. [1] [3]

The README links colored frame previews and a draw-on GIF with a reproducibility recipe. Those developer artifacts were read as documentation; no video was generated or visually graded by Scout. [1] [3]

Install

Use the official checkout, a separate Python 3.9+ environment and its requirements, plus FFmpeg with libass. Configure one supported image provider privately and inspect the project doctor output. The image provider and Edge narration are online; local tracing/rendering is CPU work. [1] [3]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3]

  1. Prepare a short script and a storyboard with concrete per-scene subjects; review the wording before requesting speech or images.
  2. Generate/review static illustrations with cleared character/style references, then use the documented staged build so individual scenes can be corrected.
  3. Inspect the draw-on/paint order and final captions. For full-frame color, review the documented paint-bg and zero-margin behavior; check the completed duration/audio rather than trusting a file’s existence.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3]

Hardware
The developer explicitly states no local generation GPU is required for this hosted-image/CPU-render route. Minimum RAM and total disk space are not published.
Software
Python 3.9+, Pillow, NumPy, edge-tts and FFmpeg/libass, a subtitle font and a supported provider key/configuration. The public project does not run a diffusion model locally.
Platforms
Output testing is explicitly documented only on Windows 11. Cross-platform code paths exist, but macOS/Linux were not tested by the author; their likely compatibility is not treated as confirmed support.

  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. Complete MIT code grant reviewed; bundled font uses SIL OFL 1.1 and example illustrations are separately declared MIT by the author. Image-provider, speech, reference-character and downstream asset terms remain independent.

Fine screens, hatching, gradients or broken outlines can disrupt region coloring. Provider free quotas change and are not a guaranteed zero-cost production plan. [1] [3]

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3]

  1. After ComfyUI and the local proxy are configured, open localhost:3000 and load a short clip with a clearly visible permitted subject.
  2. Provide a cleared character image/description, inspect the two proposed first-frame looks and approve one.
  3. Choose the Wan Fast route, render a short test and compare the synchronized before/after. Inspect face, hands, occlusion and audio; optional upscale has its own model/download.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3]

Hardware
App gates video choices below 16 GB NVIDIA VRAM. For people/pets its RAM table permits 2 s at 16 GB RAM, 8 s at 24 GB, 15 s at 32 GB and up to 30 s at 64 GB. Look models are about 31 GB; Wan route about 31 GB plus optional 16.7 GB face-pass and shared files. These are component/author limits, not total workspace minima.
Software
ComfyUI 0.34+, Git, Node.js 22+ and Python 3.10+ for comfy-api-proxy. ComfyUI, proxy (8189) and app (3000) are separate services. Models are downloaded independently into the documented folders.
Platforms
Windows NVIDIA route is documented/tested by the author. Linux instructions are explicitly untested; macOS is unsupported for these NVIDIA workflows.

Viggle/MiniMax H3 has territorial/commercial restrictions and LTX object models have community terms; those are not granted by MIT and are not the selected Wan route.
  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. Complete MIT app/workflow grant reviewed. The author lists Wan2.2 Fun Control as Apache-2.0, separately from Qwen image preparation, face models and optional upscaling. Viggle/MiniMax H3 has territorial/commercial restrictions and LTX object models have community terms; those are not granted by MIT and are not the selected Wan route.

The repository was created on 2026-10-26. This is its first full guide in this library, not independent validation of a new release.

Quality assessment: Quality unverified

Software licensing and a concrete creative AI use are supported. The remaining checks do not establish a recommendation.

Scope: Study a learned PBR-material compression/reconstruction workflow with explicit data/source identity and reproducible saved-artifact checks.

Evidence checked 2026-11-13; documentation review does not imply hands-on testing.

  • Open-source software license · verified: The complete root software license was read and archived. The grant covers this project code; unresolved model, service and asset terms are disclosed separately. [2]
  • Creative results · documented: The author reports Linux tests and short real-data runs, and distinguishes its implementation choices from the published paper. No full paper-metric reproduction or hardware real-time shader result was established in this review. [1] [3] [4]
  • Reproducible setup · documented: Prepare a compatible Linux PyTorch CUDA environment, install the requirements and compile the separate BC6H reference decoder with CMake/GCC. Build a correctly encoded nine-channel material target plus its identity/hash file before the documented short debug training run. Independent reproduction has not been established in this review. [1] [3] [4]
  • Maintenance and support · unknown: A release or active repository alone does not establish sustained support. Workflow-specific support and unresolved blockers need a deeper review.

  • Models, services and costs · documented: Complete MIT project grant and third-party notice reviewed. The retained bcdec header has its separate MIT OR Unlicense grant; material datasets and texture rights are independent. This is not the original paper authors’ code release. Local compatible GPU training, storage and separately permitted material datasets. [1] [3] [4]

Documentation review only; AI Media Scout did not install or run this software. Several optimizer/initialization/activation choices are the reproducer’s interpretations. The CLI does not consume the explanatory JSON config files. The hardware-compressed realtime shader and frame timing are not implemented/measured here, so offline save/reload is the assessed scope. Full-training status and independent visual/metric equivalence remain unverified.

How it uses AI

A per-material neural trainer optimizes block-compressed feature textures and a small MLP, exports BC6H DDS/FP16 decoder artifacts and independently reloads them for reference reconstruction. [1] [3] [4]

Introduction

A per-material neural trainer optimizes block-compressed feature textures and a small MLP, exports BC6H DDS/FP16 decoder artifacts and independently reloads them for reference reconstruction. [1] [3] [4]

What it is good for

Study a learned PBR-material compression/reconstruction workflow with explicit data/source identity and reproducible saved-artifact checks. [1] [3] [4]

Demo & examples

The author reports Linux tests and short real-data runs, and distinguishes its implementation choices from the published paper. No full paper-metric reproduction or hardware real-time shader result was established in this review. [1] [3] [4]

Install

Prepare a compatible Linux PyTorch CUDA environment, install the requirements and compile the separate BC6H reference decoder with CMake/GCC. Build a correctly encoded nine-channel material target plus its identity/hash file before the documented short debug training run. [1] [3] [4]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Prepare a compatible Linux PyTorch CUDA environment, install the requirements and compile the separate BC6H reference decoder with CMake/GCC. Build a correctly encoded nine-channel material target plus its identity/hash file before the documented short debug training run.
git clone https://github.com/zhr123321a/bcf6.git
cd bcf6
python -m pip install -r requirements.txt
cmake -S . -B build -DCMAKE_BUILD_TYPE=Release
cmake --build build -j 2
python -m bcf6.train --help

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3] [4]

  1. Prepare permitted diffuse, normal and ORM targets with the documented channel semantics, dimensions and identity SHA; do not silently linearize/rescale/fill missing channels.
  2. Use the short 45-update workflow only to check pipeline/save/reload, retaining strict-pass warnings. It is not a convergence or quality benchmark.
  3. Inspect saved DDS/decoder/metadata and replay metrics. For a longer run preserve the exact source, target and full training configuration; do not confuse the PyTorch reference reconstruction with a hardware-sampled realtime renderer.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3] [4]

Hardware
Minimum system RAM, VRAM and total disk space are not documented in the reviewed instructions.
Software
Requirements list NumPy >=1.24, Pillow >=9 and Torch >=2.2 as lower bounds, not a tested version matrix. The stated Linux validation used PyTorch 2.7.1+cu118 and GCC 11.4; CMake builds the reference decoder.
Platforms
Linux-first implementation and acceptance. Windows/MSVC instructions exist, but Windows computation and cross-platform artifact acceptance remain pending. CPU is a debug path, not the suggested full-training route.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Model weights, assets and services remain separate. [2] [1] [3] [4]

  • Weights: Complete MIT project grant and third-party notice reviewed. The retained bcdec header has its separate MIT OR Unlicense grant; material datasets and texture rights are independent. This is not the original paper authors’ code release.
  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. Complete MIT project grant and third-party notice reviewed. The retained bcdec header has its separate MIT OR Unlicense grant; material datasets and texture rights are independent. This is not the original paper authors’ code release.
  • Cost: Local compatible GPU training, storage and separately permitted material datasets.

Why it merits attention

Included for the documented creative workflow. A complete guide and popularity do not establish output reliability. [1] [3] [4]

Limitations

Several optimizer/initialization/activation choices are the reproducer’s interpretations. The CLI does not consume the explanatory JSON config files. The hardware-compressed realtime shader and frame timing are not implemented/measured here, so offline save/reload is the assessed scope. Full-training status and independent visual/metric equivalence remain unverified. [1] [3] [4]

SceneDirector

Documented creative workflow; quality confidence assessed separately · MIT · recently-created

The repository was created on 2026-11-12. This is its first full guide in this library, not independent validation of a new release.

Docs checked 2026-11-13 · 0 GitHub stars · Release v0.1.0 (2026-11-12) · Documentation review; installation not tested

Quality assessment: Quality unverified

Software licensing and a concrete creative AI use are supported. The remaining checks do not establish a recommendation.

Scope: Conversational scene assembly, lighting, camera moves and short Blender animations with explicit reference and preview approval gates.

Evidence checked 2026-11-13; documentation review does not imply hands-on testing.

  • Open-source software license · verified: The complete root software license was read and archived. The grant covers this project code; unresolved model, service and asset terms are disclosed separately. [2]
  • Creative results · documented: The author shows a five-second reading-corner camera move and desktop screenshots. These illustrate a workflow; this review did not reproduce the animation. [1]
  • Reproducible setup · documented: Clone the official repository, install Python 3.10+, then use the documented setup command. It can download Blender and install a Claude skill; review its options first. Configure a tool-capable local Ollama model or a hosted provider in a private .env. The Windows desktop executable still needs the repository and Python beside it. Independent reproduction has not been established in this review. [1]
  • Maintenance and support · unknown: A release or active repository alone does not establish sustained support. Workflow-specific support and unresolved blockers need a deeper review.
  • Independent use · unknown: Independent first-hand use or a working third-party integration was not established for this selected workflow.
  • Models, services and costs · documented: MIT covers SceneDirector code. Blender, Python packages, chosen LLM weights, asset licenses and hosted services retain their own terms. Poly Haven and ambientCG assets are documented as CC0. Claude access is separate. Local model/render compute or external LLM fees; optional Tripo/Meshy mesh generation uses credits. A provider free tier is not a permanent cost guarantee. [1]

Documentation review only; AI Media Scout did not install or run this software. Reference images are art-direction targets, not automatic full-scene 3D reconstruction. Complex hair, cloth and IK still need human work; Mixamo is manual. Paid Tripo/Meshy connectors were tested against mocks, which does not establish live provider success. Free tiers can be limited and Pollinations references may carry watermarks.

How it uses AI

A tool-calling language model directs headless Blender: it proposes references, asks for approval, assembles assets, inspects screenshots and produces editable scenes and renders. [1]

Introduction

A tool-calling language model directs headless Blender: it proposes references, asks for approval, assembles assets, inspects screenshots and produces editable scenes and renders. [1]

What it is good for

Conversational scene assembly, lighting, camera moves and short Blender animations with explicit reference and preview approval gates. [1]

Demo & examples

The author shows a five-second reading-corner camera move and desktop screenshots. These illustrate a workflow; this review did not reproduce the animation. [1]

Install

Clone the official repository, install Python 3.10+, then use the documented setup command. It can download Blender and install a Claude skill; review its options first. Configure a tool-capable local Ollama model or a hosted provider in a private .env. The Windows desktop executable still needs the repository and Python beside it. [1]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Clone the official repository, install Python 3.10+, then use the documented setup command. It can download Blender and install a Claude skill; review its options first. Configure a tool-capable local Ollama model or a hosted provider in a private .env. The Windows desktop executable still needs the repository and Python beside it.
python sd.py setup
python cli.py --doctor
python cli.py my_film

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1]

  1. Choose a reference or upload your own, then approve the look and shot plan.
  2. Inspect intermediate screenshots, scale, camera and lighting; approve a low-resolution preview.
  3. Render the final still or animation, then continue editing the saved scene.blend if necessary.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1]

Hardware
Minimum system RAM, VRAM and total disk space are not documented in the reviewed instructions.
Software
Python 3.10+, requests, Blender 3.6+ (author tested 5.3), FFmpeg on PATH for MP4, and a tool-calling LLM. Building the optional Tauri UI needs Node 18+ and Rust.
Platforms
Windows has a documented unsigned desktop executable. Portable Blender auto-download is documented for Windows and Linux x64. A Mac desktop binary or tested Mac setup is not established by these instructions.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Model weights, assets and services remain separate. [2] [1] [3]

  • Weights: MIT covers SceneDirector code. Blender, Python packages, chosen LLM weights, asset licenses and hosted services retain their own terms. Poly Haven and ambientCG assets are documented as CC0. Claude access is separate.
  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. MIT covers SceneDirector code. Blender, Python packages, chosen LLM weights, asset licenses and hosted services retain their own terms. Poly Haven and ambientCG assets are documented as CC0. Claude access is separate.
  • Cost: Local model/render compute or external LLM fees; optional Tripo/Meshy mesh generation uses credits. A provider free tier is not a permanent cost guarantee.

Why it merits attention

Included for the documented creative workflow. A complete guide and popularity do not establish output reliability. [1]

Limitations

Reference images are art-direction targets, not automatic full-scene 3D reconstruction. Complex hair, cloth and IK still need human work; Mixamo is manual. Paid Tripo/Meshy connectors were tested against mocks, which does not establish live provider success. Free tiers can be limited and Pollinations references may carry watermarks. [1]

Photon AI Studio companion

Scope: Selection-aware image edits added as masked layers, canvas expansion and local AI super-resolution inside an existing Photon Studio workflow.

Evidence checked 2026-11-13; documentation review does not imply hands-on testing.

  • Open-source software license · verified: The complete root software license was read and archived. The grant covers this project code; unresolved model, service and asset terms are disclosed separately. [2]
  • Creative results · documented: The author provides before/after canoe inpainting, forest outpainting and a winter landscape transformation using RealVisXL. These are author examples, not an independent evaluation. [1]
  • Reproducible setup · documented: Install Photon Studio separately and enable its automation CLI. Use an OS-specific companion release or run the Node source. Its wizard installs a private Python/ComfyUI/model stack or can reuse an existing ComfyUI installation; review downloads and model choices before accepting. Independent reproduction has not been established in this review. [1]
  • Maintenance and support · unknown: A release or active repository alone does not establish sustained support. Workflow-specific support and unresolved blockers need a deeper review.

ComfyUI/nodes and downloaded RealVisXL, Fooocus, IP-Adapter, Illustrious and Z-Image weights have separate licenses; several model terms are deferred to model cards. Companion code has no license fee. Photon Studio access, local hardware and any optional service remain separate. [1]

Documentation review only; AI Media Scout did not install or run this software. This does not make the required Photon Studio host open-source. The broad neural-filter list and local-only privacy claims are developer documentation, not verified feature parity or a network audit. Unsigned releases and missing model-card reviews remain installation/rights gaps.

How it uses AI

An open-source local companion connects Photon Studio selection/layer automation to ComfyUI for neural inpainting, outpainting, generation, upscaling and filters. [1]

Introduction

An open-source local companion connects Photon Studio selection/layer automation to ComfyUI for neural inpainting, outpainting, generation, upscaling and filters. [1]

What it is good for

Selection-aware image edits added as masked layers, canvas expansion and local AI super-resolution inside an existing Photon Studio workflow. [1]

Demo & examples

The author provides before/after canoe inpainting, forest outpainting and a winter landscape transformation using RealVisXL. These are author examples, not an independent evaluation. [1]

Install

Install Photon Studio separately and enable its automation CLI. Use an OS-specific companion release or run the Node source. Its wizard installs a private Python/ComfyUI/model stack or can reuse an existing ComfyUI installation; review downloads and model choices before accepting. [1]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Install Photon Studio separately and enable its automation CLI. Use an OS-specific companion release or run the Node source. Its wizard installs a private Python/ComfyUI/model stack or can reuse an existing ComfyUI installation; review downloads and model choices before accepting.
node src/server.js
node src/server.js --no-window --port=7788

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1]

  1. Open a document in Photon and select a region or canvas.
  2. Choose a model pack and a fill, expand, generate or upscale tool.
  3. Inspect the resulting layer, soft mask and seam; retain or discard variations.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1]

Hardware
Author recommendation: NVIDIA 8 GB+ VRAM (6 GB low-VRAM mode) or Apple Silicon 16 GB+ unified memory, preferably 32 GB; 16 GB RAM and 25–50 GB free disk. Base pack ~8.4 GB; Z-Image Turbo ~19.4 GB and 12 GB+ VRAM. These are documented recommendations, not independently tested minima.
Software
Photon Studio with its CLI enabled; Node 22+ for source use. The private installer documents Python 3.12 and PyTorch CUDA 12.8, Metal or CPU.
Platforms
Documented release filenames target Windows x64, macOS arm64 and Linux x64. CPU mode is documented as slow; platform-specific filter reliability was not independently tested.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Model weights, assets and services remain separate. [2] [1]

  • Weights: MIT covers the companion, not proprietary Photon Studio. ComfyUI/nodes and downloaded RealVisXL, Fooocus, IP-Adapter, Illustrious and Z-Image weights have separate licenses; several model terms are deferred to model cards.
  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. MIT covers the companion, not proprietary Photon Studio. ComfyUI/nodes and downloaded RealVisXL, Fooocus, IP-Adapter, Illustrious and Z-Image weights have separate licenses; several model terms are deferred to model cards.
  • Cost: Companion code has no license fee. Photon Studio access, local hardware and any optional service remain separate.

Why it merits attention

Included for the documented creative workflow. A complete guide and popularity do not establish output reliability. [1]

Limitations

This does not make the required Photon Studio host open-source. The broad neural-filter list and local-only privacy claims are developer documentation, not verified feature parity or a network audit. Unsigned releases and missing model-card reviews remain installation/rights gaps. [1]

How it uses AI

ZigZag exposes its embroidery design canvas to AI agents over MCP, and its Ask Agent panel lets an authenticated Claude Code or Codex session make visible design edits. [1] [3]

Introduction

ZigZag exposes its embroidery design canvas to AI agents over MCP, and its Ask Agent panel lets an authenticated Claude Code or Codex session make visible design edits. [1] [3]

What it is good for

AI-assisted badge composition, lettering and vector edits, followed by conventional stitch planning and PES/DST/EXP/JEF/VP3 export. [1] [3]

Demo & examples

The README illustrates studio stitch previews and an agent prompt for a 50 mm mountain badge. A preview is not evidence of a successful physical sew-out. [1] [3]

Install

Use the documented Mac or Windows release, or build the Rust source. Open the studio and connect an already authenticated agent through the localhost MCP endpoint; a bridge is supplied for stdio clients. GUI/Bluetooth source builds use their feature flags and platform libraries. [1] [3]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Use the documented Mac or Windows release, or build the Rust source. Open the studio and connect an already authenticated agent through the localhost MCP endpoint; a bridge is supplied for stdio clients. GUI/Bluetooth source builds use their feature flags and platform libraries.
cargo build --release --features gui,ble
claude mcp add --transport http zigzag http://127.0.0.1:7878/mcp

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3]

  1. Set fabric, hoop and physical dimensions before asking the agent to compose a small design.
  2. Inspect stitches, density and Issues, then adjust spacing, underlay and sequence.
  3. Export the machine format and perform a small fabric test before a final garment.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3]

Hardware
Minimum system RAM, VRAM and total disk space are not documented in the reviewed instructions.
Software
Rust toolchain for source builds; Linux GUI requires X11/Wayland/OpenGL libraries. An authenticated supported AI client is separate. Bluetooth is needed only for the documented Brother Skitch PP1 connection.
Platforms
macOS 11+ releases are documented for Intel and Apple Silicon. Windows 10 x64 version 1709+ and Windows 11 releases are documented. Linux is documented as a source build.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Model weights, assets and services remain separate. [2] [1] [3]

  • Commercial: Software use follows Apache-2.0. The complete Apache-2.0 grant and NOTICE were reviewed. Vendored/ported MIT and Apache code, fonts and other dependencies retain their notices. Agent subscriptions or local model terms are separate.
  • Cost: Local compute plus any chosen external model or service; provider access and charges are separate.

Documentation review only; AI Media Scout did not install or run this software. Timing and appearance claims are developer results. This is an optimization pipeline, not a ready-to-print haptic fabrication service; independent first-hand use and maintenance checks remain incomplete.

How it uses AI

A neural optimization workflow combines visual and tactile guidance to synthesize mesh appearance and high-frequency tactile normal maps. [1]

Introduction

A neural optimization workflow combines visual and tactile guidance to synthesize mesh appearance and high-frequency tactile normal maps. [1]

What it is good for

Researching touch-informed 3D materials, single-texture optimization and multi-part object appearance, with mesh and texture outputs for further rendering. [1]

Demo & examples

The project publishes visual comparisons, debug videos and exported meshes/texture maps. A tactile normal map does not demonstrate printable surface displacement or a reproduced physical sensation. [1]

Install

Follow the documented Linux conda/PyTorch environment, install repository dependencies and the tactileDreamfusion branch of Blender-render-toolkit, then obtain the separately documented data/models. The scripts have separate training and visualization modes. [1]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Follow the documented Linux conda/PyTorch environment, install repository dependencies and the tactileDreamfusion branch of Blender-render-toolkit, then obtain the separately documented data/models. The scripts have separate training and visualization modes.
bash scripts/single_texture_generation.sh -train
bash scripts/single_texture_generation.sh

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1]

  1. Prepare the documented mesh, texture and tactile inputs.
  2. Run a small single-texture training case and inspect logs before multi-part work.
  3. Visualize the output and inspect OBJ/MTL, albedo and tactile-normal maps together.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1]

Hardware
Paper/developer examples use an NVIDIA A6000 with 48 GB VRAM. The README describes a 24 GB configuration with reduced single-texture patch batch size; that is not a universal minimum. RAM and total disk minima are not documented.
Software
Tested environment: Linux, Python 3.10.13, PyTorch 2.2.1 and CUDA 12.1; Blender-render-toolkit uses the named project branch.
Platforms
Linux is the documented tested environment. Mac and Windows support are not established by these instructions.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Model weights, assets and services remain separate. [2] [1]

  • Weights: MIT covers this project code. Borrowed components, TextureDreamBooth/DreamGaussian/DiffSeg dependencies, model weights and tactile data have separate terms that this review has not completely resolved.
  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. MIT covers this project code. Borrowed components, TextureDreamBooth/DreamGaussian/DiffSeg dependencies, model weights and tactile data have separate terms that this review has not completely resolved.
  • Cost: Local compute plus any chosen external model or service; provider access and charges are separate.

Why it merits attention

Included for the documented creative workflow. A complete guide and popularity do not establish output reliability. [1]

Limitations

Timing and appearance claims are developer results. This is an optimization pipeline, not a ready-to-print haptic fabrication service; independent first-hand use and maintenance checks remain incomplete. [1]

The full software license was reviewed. Model weights, assets and services remain separate. [2] [1]

  • Weights: MIT covers project code; vendor MDM/EDGE/ARIA retain their own grants. MOSA is documented as CC BY 4.0 and is downloaded separately. Checkpoint, music and character-asset terms still need their own review.
  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. MIT covers project code; vendor MDM/EDGE/ARIA retain their own grants. MOSA is documented as CC BY 4.0 and is downloaded separately. Checkpoint, music and character-asset terms still need their own review.
  • Cost: Local preprocessing/inference; optional from-scratch training is documented as days on one GPU, not a cost or duration guarantee.

Why it merits attention

Included for the documented creative workflow. A complete guide and popularity do not establish output reliability. [1]

Limitations

MIDI is reconstructed from annotations and lacks per-note velocity. Only Pianoid checkpoints are distributed; baseline rows require training. Source uses torch.load with weights_only=False, so checkpoint provenance matters. This review did not reproduce the paper numbers. The archived environment.yml still names m2b/Python 3.10, while the actual setup.sh creates pianoid/Python 3.11; this guide follows the latter documented route. The source installer has a CPU fallback, which does not establish practical CPU reproduction of the GPU evaluation. [1]

How it uses AI

An unofficial port runs a four-step MOSS image transformer on an AMD XDNA 2 NPU, with CPU text encoding and mixed CPU/NPU image decoding. [1]

Introduction

An unofficial port runs a four-step MOSS image transformer on an AMD XDNA 2 NPU, with CPU text encoding and mixed CPU/NPU image decoding. [1]

What it is good for

Exploring explicitly documented 512-square and 1024-square text-to-image inference on a particular Ryzen AI machine without claiming all-NPU execution. [1]

Demo & examples

The author supplies image samples, per-stage logs and repeated timing measurements on one Ryzen AI 7 PRO 350. Reported text rendering includes misspellings, and identical prompts/seeds differ from the official bf16 pipeline. [1]

Install

Install AMD Ryzen AI Software 1.8.0 and its driver, conda/Miniforge, PowerShell 7 and Git. The size-specific setup creates two environments and downloads pinned/hash-checked model parts; run the matching doctor command before the first example. [1]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Install AMD Ryzen AI Software 1.8.0 and its driver, conda/Miniforge, PowerShell 7 and Git. The size-specific setup creates two environments and downloads pinned/hash-checked model parts; run the matching doctor command before the first example.
pwsh -NoProfile -File .\setup.ps1 -Size 512
pwsh -NoProfile -File .\doctor.ps1 -Size 512
pwsh -NoProfile -File .\gen.ps1 -Items .\examples\one.json -Out .\outputs\first -Size 512

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1]

  1. Choose the supported size and inspect the pinned model manifest and local paths.
  2. Generate one documented JSON prompt and inspect PNG, latents and per-stage logs.
  3. Batch several prompts to amortize loading, respecting the documented token limits and exit codes.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1]

Hardware
Author-tested AMD Ryzen AI 7 PRO 350 with 32 GB RAM. Documented free-disk requirement about 50 GB for 512 only and 54 GB for both sizes; 16 GB RAM was not tested. No separate GPU/VRAM minimum is established.
Software
Verified author configuration: Windows 11 Home build 26200, Ryzen AI Software 1.8.0, NPU driver 32.0.203.329. Text environment uses Python 3.12.16 and a separately pinned CPU PyTorch stack.
Platforms
Only the stated Windows/XDNA 2 machine/driver configuration was tested by the author. Other XDNA 2 CPUs, 16 GB machines, other drivers, Mac and Linux are not established.

  • Weights: Apache-2.0 covers scripts. The README identifies the MOSS model, four-step LoRA and AMD FLUX decoder as Apache-2.0; these are separate artifacts and their full individual grants were not all independently rechecked here. AMD runtime licensing is separate.
  • Commercial: Software use follows Apache-2.0. Apache-2.0 covers scripts. The README identifies the MOSS model, four-step LoRA and AMD FLUX decoder as Apache-2.0; these are separate artifacts and their full individual grants were not all independently rechecked here. AMD runtime licensing is separate.

How it uses AI

A local asset-library browser adds optional vision-model pack tags and SigLIP 2 semantic image search to editable license/provenance curation. [1]

Introduction

A local asset-library browser adds optional vision-model pack tags and SigLIP 2 semantic image search to editable license/provenance curation. [1]

What it is good for

Find owned game art, sprites, audio and 3D packs by meaning, retain human corrections and copy selected assets into a project with a license/credit manifest. [1]

Demo & examples

The author links a video walkthrough and interface screenshots. Search matches and model tags are suggestions; they do not verify a pack license or prove that an asset is human-made. [1]

Install

Install the Python 3.12+ application with uv/pipx/pip. Initialize and index your own library, then browse locally. Add the optional AI extra for in-process embeddings; pack tagging instead uses a separately configured vision-capable OpenAI-compatible server. [1]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Install the Python 3.12+ application with uv/pipx/pip. Initialize and index your own library, then browse locally. Add the optional AI extra for in-process embeddings; pack tagging instead uses a separately configured vision-capable OpenAI-compatible server.
uv tool install "assetcat[ai]"
assetcat init ~/Assets
assetcat index
assetcat serve --open
assetcat classify --kind embed

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1]

  1. Inspect inferred pack boundaries, then enter actual licenses and provenance evidence in curation.
  2. Sample model tagging before a full pass; review and correct suggested tags.
  3. Use meaning/similarity search, inspect matching files, then pull permitted assets with their credit manifest.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1]

Hardware
Embedding model download about 1.5 GB; an example 200,000-image vector index about 350 MB plus thumbnails/database. Suggested vision-model downloads are about 5 or 16 GB. Universal RAM/VRAM and total working-disk minima are not documented.
Software
Python 3.12+; PyTorch for the AI extra. SigLIP 2 downloads on first use, with CPU, Apple Silicon or NVIDIA execution. Pack tagging uses LM Studio, Ollama, llama.cpp or a separately authorized cloud endpoint.
Platforms
macOS, Windows and Linux are documented. Windows NVIDIA use needs a CUDA-compatible PyTorch build rather than assuming default CPU packages provide GPU execution.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Model weights, assets and services remain separate. [2] [1]

  • Weights: The full project code grant was reviewed. Gemma/SigLIP model terms, LM Studio or other host access and every indexed asset license remain separate. User-entered provenance is evidence-backed curation, not automatic legal clearance.
  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. The full project code grant was reviewed. Gemma/SigLIP model terms, LM Studio or other host access and every indexed asset license remain separate. User-entered provenance is evidence-backed curation, not automatic legal clearance.
  • Cost: Local compute plus optional external model/service use; owning/indexing a pack does not establish permission for every project.

Included for the documented creative workflow. A complete guide and popularity do not establish output reliability. [1]

Excluded and unresolved findings

These findings are retained as research notes and are not part of the eligible tool library.

  • HairGS · excluded: The root MIT file does not supersede the additional non-commercial Gaussian-Splatting terms that the README says apply to the combined distribution. It is creative research context, not an admitted open-source application. Source
  • DiffLocks · excluded: The complete grant limits use to non-commercial scientific/educational/artistic purposes and restricts redistribution. A separately MIT-licensed subcomponent does not make the complete project open-source. Source
  • Crayotter · excluded: The full root grant is PolyForm Noncommercial; paper wording about openness does not replace that software restriction. Source
  • IPHG / CT2Hair / HairPort · excluded: The inspected root grants are CC BY-NC or CC BY-NC-ND. These non-commercial/no-derivatives restrictions do not qualify as open-source software grants. Source Source Source
  • SAFE-Hair / NeuralHDHair / HairGPT · needs-license-review: A promised release or paper/project page does not establish a complete released and licensed application. Actual inference availability and full grants remain unresolved for these candidates. Source Source Source
  • HyFluid / PICT smoke · needs-license-review: Actual research code was located, but complete software grants were not returned for the inspected repositories. Smoke reconstruction examples were not substituted for license evidence. Source Source
  • VRMHairMaker · needs-license-review: The project has HairDeck-related implementation but no complete own software grant was returned. An upstream Apache grant is not automatically a grant for the wrapper. Source
  • StrandHead · needs-license-review: An MIT root sits alongside borrowed NeuralHaircut material with separate non-commercial terms. The full combined source/provenance scope requires resolution before admission. Source
  • FengLairh ComfyUI-HairDeck distribution · needs-license-review: The adapter root is Apache-2.0, but this distribution vendors SAM 3 and DINOv3 software under custom licenses covering code as well as weights, including use restrictions. It is held pending a clearly separated open-code scope; mirrors and checksum validation do not remove license conditions. Source Source Source
  • Comfy-WebUI / wan-pilot · needs-license-review: The full Apache-looking grants alter the standard liability wording. They were not automatically treated as recognized Apache-2.0 merely because metadata identifies that SPDX value. Source Source
  • Mia full-guide completion · needs-license-review: The application root is Apache, but bundled model/graph components have separate community and open-source grants and some models are non-commercial or research-limited. The existing historical screened entry is preserved; this edition does not assert an all-Apache bundle or publish a completed guide without resolving the selected component scope. Source
  • AgenticCAD · excluded: The primary page explicitly states free for non-commercial use. Creative relevance and CAD export do not establish an open-source software grant. Source
  • Xotion Studio · excluded: The complete GitLab grant is PolyForm Noncommercial, so this otherwise relevant video project is excluded from open-source software results. Source
  • AEGIR / Fol.AI / PHOSA / FORM · needs-license-review: The primary pages describe relevant research but announce code/data as coming soon or leave release availability unresolved. Paper/video examples are not evidence that a licensed implementation has shipped. Source Source Source Source
  • RiTa v4 · excluded: The primary site explicitly describes the toolkit as AI-free. It is useful generative-language context, but conventional language generation alone does not meet this library AI-only scope. Source
  • Missing or unreleased implementations · needs-license-review: Some searched repositories contained only architecture/docs, planned neural features or screenshots/binary releases. Those were not admitted merely from an AI label, root license or executable download; raw candidate details remain local for later source checks. Source
  • MoneyPrinterPlus · excluded: The complete root LICENSE appends personal/educational-only and no-commercial-use conditions to a GPL-looking text. These restrictions exclude the combined grant despite GitHub GPL metadata. Source
  • OpenDesign · needs-license-review: The full license changes Apache trademark wording and the additional-liability/support section. Its Apache metadata and large star count were not substituted for a recognized unmodified grant review. Source
  • TeaCache composite grant · needs-license-review: The full root license includes a large composite set of upstream notices/grants. A complete composite review remains pending; the first Apache header was not treated as the whole license. Source
  • No-Slop Color · excluded: The official current repository metadata marks this project archived. It is not admitted as a currently maintained discovery; the reviewed MIT license and creative-use source evidence remain retained locally. Archive status alone does not revoke its license or judge all historical output. Source
